# Colorimetric lateral flow assays for virus detection: sections 3.1-3.6 (working draft, 2026-11-10)

Sections A-F as rewritten around the colorimetric signal.

---

# Introduction paragraph: origin and principle of the lateral flow assay (draft v0.1)

Status: the principle and the uses below are supported by papers read for this review. The historical statements (first descriptions, first commercial products, dates and inventors) are NOT yet included because no verified historical source has been retrieved; PubMed search was unstable in this session. They are marked [HISTORY: source needed] and must not be filled from memory.

Text:

The lateral flow assay (LFA) combines the binding of antibodies to a target with chromatographic separation of the complexes on a porous membrane, so that a drop of sample moving by capillary force forms a coloured zone when the target is present (DOI: 10.3390/bios11070229). [HISTORY: first descriptions of immunochromatographic strips and the adoption of coloured particles as labels - source needed.] Gold nanoparticles became the most widely used label because they are simple to synthesise, easy to couple to antibodies and give a visible red signal (DOI: 10.3390/bios11070229; DOI: 10.1016/j.bios.2022.114094). Strips of this kind are now familiar as self-tests for pregnancy, influenza and malaria (DOI: 10.1016/j.bios.2022.114385), and they are valued because they meet most of the WHO ASSURED criteria: affordable, sensitive, specific, user-friendly, rapid and robust, equipment-free and deliverable to end users (DOI: 10.1016/j.bios.2022.114385; DOI: 10.1038/s41467-023-37018-6). [HISTORY: development from latex and gold agglutination tests to the first commercial rapid tests - source needed.] Their main limitation, the high limit of detection compared with laboratory methods, is the problem addressed by the technologies reviewed in sections 3.1 to 3.7 (DOI: 10.3390/bios11070229).

# Part A (working text v5): gold labels and the colorimetric signal

Working text, 2026-11-10. This is the first part of the review. Its question, and that of every later part, is how each strategy, material or technology changes the colour signal of a lateral flow strip for virus detection, by how much within the study that reports it, and at what cost. Each study is described by the strategy it used and by the improvement that its abstract (or full text, where read) reports against its own baseline; the target virus is named for every method. Numbers are in the tables, and Table A4 gathers the within-paper changes in the colour signal. † = read in full; all other rows are abstract only (NR = not reported in the abstract). ‡ = a second readout is also reported; only the colorimetric channel is counted. Every DOI cited in the text appears in a table and in the evidence table.

# 3.1 Gold-based labels: how engineering changes the colorimetric signal

## 3.1.0 The colour signal of a strip, and how this review is organised

In a colorimetric lateral flow immunoassay (LFIA) the result is the colour that builds up at the test line. The strength of that colour depends on three things: how many particles the sandwich captures, how strongly each captured particle changes the white membrane, and how clearly that change can be seen against the background. Gold nanoparticles (AuNPs) became the default label because they absorb visible light strongly, are easy to make and are easy to couple to antibodies (DOI: 10.1016/j.bios.2022.114094). Their weakness is a high limit of detection (LOD) when only a few particles reach the line (DOI: 10.3390/bios11070229).

The review follows the chain from the label to the reading. This first part deals with gold, the standard label, and with what has been done to make its colour stronger. Part 3.2 deals with labels other than gold, 3.3 with amplification after capture, 3.4 with changes to the strip, the flow and the sample, 3.5 with amplifying the nucleic acid before the strip, and 3.6 with the reading of the colour by readers, smartphones and algorithms. In each part the question is the same, and the answer is given as the change that a paper reports against its own conventional strip.

This part is arranged by what the authors did to the gold label. Tuning the particle and what it carries comes first (3.1.1), then making the captured gold more visible after the immunoreaction (3.1.2). Structures with a larger optical effect per particle follow (3.1.3), including gold-based structures that also carry a second signal, for which only the colour channel is counted (3.1.4). The binder or antigen is next (3.1.5), then the assay format (3.1.6), then a change of optics (3.1.7), and finally the conventional gold strips that apply none of these changes and serve as baseline (3.1.8). Section 3.1.9 states what the evidence supports. The part covers 119 included records, all from 2015-2026.

**How the signal is read.** Reading the abstracts of the 119 records in this part, 49 state that the strip is read by eye, 1 state that a device is used, 5 state either eye or device, 63 describe a strip without saying how it is read, and 1 has no abstract. 29 records (the 26 gold-based labels of 3.1.4 and three others) report a second readout or a non-colour comparator; they are marked ‡, and only the colorimetric channel is counted. "Not stated" means only that the abstract is silent. Records read in full give their reading mode from the full text (marked "full text" in the tables); the counts above refer to abstracts.

## 3.1.1 Getting the standard particle right: size and surface

The first design question is the particle itself. Large AuNPs carry more colour, but they also move more slowly through the membrane and may not reach the test line in useful numbers (DOI: 10.3390/bios11070229; DOI: 10.1038/s41467-025-58663-z). Studies that vary particle diameter treat this trade-off directly: a hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg) strip was built around a series of particle sizes (34.2-137.8 nm), and a dengue virus NS1 strip around large (80 nm) particles (DOI: 10.3390/s16122154; DOI: 10.3390/diagnostics16030395).

What sits on the gold matters as much as the gold itself. Oriented conjugation lets an antibody bind through a defined site and reduces the amount of antibody needed, shown on an HBsAg strip for hepatitis B virus (DOI: 10.1016/j.aca.2021.338623). An additive that chelates the gold surface (EDTA-K2) improved a SARS-CoV-2 IgM/IgG antibody test without raising false positives (DOI: 10.2147/ijn.s281594). Studies of the protein corona and of the aggregation state of the probe show that the layer of adsorbed protein changes how the probe binds in NS1 sandwich assays for Zika virus and dengue virus (one of the three is a dengue model system) (DOI: 10.1021/acs.bioconjchem.6b00523; DOI: 10.3390/pharmaceutics14112439; DOI: 10.1021/acsami.0c08628).

These studies are valuable because they explain why nominally identical strips perform differently. Most of them, however, are mechanistic, and their abstracts report few diagnostic data (Table A1a). Even a well-tuned conventional strip reaches a limit, which leads to the next approach: making more of the gold that has already been captured.

Several further studies change how the antibody is attached to the gold or what else is on the particle. A comparison of antibody-conjugation strategies in dengue NS1 sandwich assays is mechanistic and gives no values in its abstract (DOI: 10.1080/15321819.2016.1269338). Bacterial protein A and protein G on gold were used as flexible detectors for SARS-CoV-2 antibodies and tested on 105 sera (DOI: 10.1007/s00216-022-03939-2), and a recombinant streptavidin fusion protein served as the signal reporter in a hepatitis C virus antibody strip tested on patient sera (DOI: 10.1002/jcla.22701). The particle itself is varied: laser-ablated gold nanoparticles for the SARS-CoV-2 spike antigen (DOI: 10.5599/admet.2079); hollow gold nanoparticles, where the interaction between particle and antibody was studied to sensitise detection of the HIV p24 antigen (DOI: 10.1039/d4ra05277c); and gold magnetic nanoparticles in a TORCH IgM strip (rubella virus, cytomegalovirus and herpes simplex virus), which reported 100% sensitivity and specificity in 162/162 sera and higher sensitivity than commercial colloidal-gold strips, with the value not given in the abstract (DOI: 10.3892/ijmm.2015.2333). Ligands matched to the target include an oseltamivir derivative on gold that distinguished oseltamivir-resistant from wild-type influenza virus (DOI: 10.1038/s41598-018-31311-x), dextrin-capped gold in a nucleic-acid biosensor for dengue-1 RNA with a cut-off of 1.2 x 10^4 PFU/mL (DOI: 10.3390/diagnostics9030074), and gold-oligonucleotide nanoconstructs designed for conserved enterovirus sequences, whose abstract reports detection of 96% of known enteroviruses (DOI: 10.3390/bios11070238). A thermoplasmonic gold-nanoprism lateral flow assay for SARS-CoV-2 RNA is listed here as a boundary case, because its signal is read from heat and not from colour alone (DOI: 10.1002/smll.202514946).

The limit addressed in 3.1.1 is therefore the number of active particles at the line. The cost is that gains from conjugation chemistry are hard to see in abstracts, because few of these papers give a value against their own baseline strip.

## 3.1.2 Making more of the captured gold: enlargement and amplification

After the sandwich has formed, the particles at the test line can act as seeds. Depositing more metal on them enlarges them, and larger or more irregular particles colour the line more strongly (DOI: 10.3390/bios11070229). Panferov and colleagues compared silver enhancement, a new gold-after-silver step based on galvanic replacement, and gold enhancement on one strip format for the SARS-CoV-2 receptor-binding domain with the same antibodies and the same AuNPs. Gold enhancement gave the lowest LOD, by more than two orders of magnitude over the unenhanced strip, and used stable reagents, but it brought nonspecific colouring and needed an optical instrument to reach the lowest value (DOI: 10.3390/bios11070229). The pattern repeats in other studies: growing 20 nm particles into much larger ones after the assay, which for potato virus X in potato leaf extracts turned most of the conventional strip's false negatives into positives (DOI: 10.1007/s00604-018-3052-7), a self-enhancement design for the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein in saliva (DOI: 10.1016/j.snb.2023.133898), and gold nanoflowers combined with gold staining for the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein, which were tested in buffer and artificial saliva only (DOI: 10.1007/s00604-024-06502-1). For two sugarcane mosaic-associated viruses, positively charged cysteamine-gold nanoparticles served as a stable colourimetric label and a further nanogold signal enhancement brought the LOD from a linear range of 10^-6 to 10^-9 g/mL down to 10^-12 g/mL (DOI: 10.1038/s41598-022-07950-6). Each lowers the LOD within its own strip format. Each also adds a step beyond the conventional run, and the lowest values often rely on calibration or a reader.

A different route is to build up the label at the line instead of growing it. Layer-by-layer assembly of AuNPs through biotin-streptavidin binding, driven by electrophoresis, lowered the LOD of a hepatitis B surface antigen strip by more than 60-fold (DOI: 10.1002/anie.202215548). The design is a two-stage workflow: the conventional strip is read first, and enhancement is applied only to samples that look negative or weakly positive. On 118 clinical samples this raised diagnostic sensitivity against a laboratory chemiluminescent assay without changing specificity, but it needs a small power supply, a holder and an extra reagent step (DOI: 10.1002/anie.202215548). In SARS-CoV-2 antibody (serology) strips, a complex of antibody-gold and antigen-gold conjugates increased the test-line signal over antigen-gold alone, and brought the visual limit to the cut-off of the reference ELISA (DOI: 10.1002/gch2.202200008).

The trade-off in this group is consistent. The gains are real and often large, but they are paid for with extra reagents, extra time, more background or dependence on instrument reading (Table A1b). That limits their use where the strip must work with no equipment. The alternative is to design a better particle in advance.

## 3.1.3 Better particles: clusters, dimers, shells and composites

The optical response of a particle depends on its size, shape, composition and the distance to its neighbours. Several groups exploit this to give each captured particle more effect on the line.

Clusters add absorption by putting several particles at each binding event. The difficulty is plasmon coupling: when particles sit closer than about 10 nm the absorption shifts towards the near-infrared and the visible colour fades (DOI: 10.1016/j.bios.2022.114094). Streptavidin-core clusters held about 15 nm apart by biotinylated antibodies, tested on the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein, avoid this and keep the red colour, and the 40-40 cluster outperformed single particles, whereas the 40-15 cluster did not (DOI: 10.1016/j.bios.2022.114094). Gold dendrimer nanochains with a red-to-blue shift have also been tested for HIV p24 (DOI: 10.1039/c7tb01394a). Core-satellite SiO2@Au particles, in which a silica core carries many small gold satellites, lowered the LOD by two orders of magnitude against 40 nm gold colloids with the same assay, although only recombinant SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein and inactivated culture virus were tested and the visual limit was much higher than the calculated LOD (DOI: 10.1021/acsami.2c13303).

Dimers use the opposite logic. Two particles joined with a sub-nanometre gap couple strongly and show two plasmon peaks, giving a stronger signal than single spheres, and they gave a clearly lower naked-eye LOD than a commercial strip for porcine epidemic diarrhea virus, with full agreement with RT-PCR on faecal samples (DOI: 10.1007/s00604-023-06013-5). The authors note that dimers are hard to prepare at scale and that their long-term stability has not been tested (DOI: 10.1007/s00604-023-06013-5).

Hollow alloy shells widen the absorption band. Silver-rich Ag-Au nanoshells have two plasmon peaks and a larger integrated absorbance than same-size AuNPs. Used as label in a bovine viral diarrhea virus (BVDV) strip, they gave a lower visual LOD than the authors' own AuNP strip with the same antibodies, and good agreement with a commercial ELISA on field samples (DOI: 10.1007/s00604-026-08241-x).

Composite particles combine gold with a second material, for example a magnetic Fe3O4 core used as a performance enhancer in an avian influenza H7 strip (DOI: 10.1039/c8ra00185e) or magnetic-gold nanostars for the Rift Valley fever virus nucleoprotein, which add magnetic pre-concentration to a spiked plasmonic shell and gave a very large gain over the authors' own gold-sphere strip in buffer and spiked serum, but require a magnet and a pre-incubation step and were not tested on patient samples (DOI: 10.1021/acsami.5c21544). One study used two-wavelength imaging of composite particles for the HIV capsid protein, which gives a large gain but depends on an imager rather than the eye (DOI: 10.1016/j.bios.2022.114133).

The strongest evidence in this group comes from papers that compare their particle with a conventional strip made with the same antibodies (clusters, shells). Others compare with commercial strips, which mixes label and antibody effects (dimers), or have been tested only in buffer (nanostars) (Table A1c). All of these designs still read absorbed colour against a white membrane. One study changes that.

## 3.1.4 Gold-based structures that also carry a second signal: what the colour channel gains

A large group of recent studies builds a structured gold label that serves two signals at once: the colour of the line, and a second signal (SERS, photothermal or fluorescence) read with an instrument. Their headline sensitivities usually come from the second signal. Under the rule of this review only the colour channel is counted, so each study is described by the structure it built and by what its colour channel reports; the second-channel figures are given in the tables, marked ‡, and are not counted. The same structures also show which engineering choices change the colour itself.

**Shaped gold: crowns, cages, rods, stars and plates.** The strategy is to change the shape of the gold so that each particle absorbs more light or absorbs at a more useful wavelength. A gold nanocrown, a gold shell decorated with external nanospheres, gave an intense dark-blue colorimetric signal for the SARS-CoV-2 spike S1 protein, with a colorimetric LOD of 91.24 pg/mL (DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.3c04361). Gold nanocages, whose size and core-to-shell gap were tuned, gave a 7-fold gain in colorimetric sensitivity over spherical gold nanoparticles for influenza A (H1N1) (1.8 ng/mL), together with an over 8000-fold gain in the photothermal channel that is not counted (DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.4c05384). Gold nanorods with a tunable absorption peak detected the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein by eye at 2 ng/mL, and 40 nasal swabs matched nucleic acid detection in 87.5% (DOI: 10.1039/d4ay00347k). One-pot Au@Pt nanostars detected antibodies to the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein at 1 ng/mL in the colorimetric mode, which the authors state is two orders of magnitude lower than commercial colloidal gold strips (DOI: 10.1016/j.aca.2024.342241). Ag-coated gold nanostars detected influenza A antibody at 0.1 ng/mL by colour (DOI: 10.1016/j.talanta.2024.127351), and Ag@Au nanoplates gave a very low background for the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein, with the photothermal limit 25-fold lower than the colorimetric one and 35 clinical samples tested (DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.3c05787). Gold-silver alloy hollow nanoshells served as a tri-mode label for SARS-CoV-2 neutralising antibodies in a competitive format, with a quantitative limit of 20 ng/mL (mode not stated) and 79 positives among serum from 98 vaccinated volunteers (DOI: 10.1016/j.aca.2023.341102).

**Silver-gold core-shell particles.** The strategy is to combine silver's stronger absorption with a gold shell that keeps the particle stable and easy to conjugate. Ag(MBA)@Au particles with a gold shell of about 2 nm (read in full) keep the stability of gold while the silver core adds absorption: in a SARS-CoV-2 IgG strip the T line became visible to the naked eye at 10^-7 mg/mL in buffer and 10^-6 mg/mL in serum, whereas the SERS channel that is not counted reached 0.22 and 0.52 pg/mL; the abstract calls this four orders of magnitude lower than visual reading, but the full-text values correspond to about 2.7 and 3.3 orders. In 98 vaccinated volunteers the clear colour band identified 26 strong positives, 6 more weak positives were found only by Raman, and the commercial colloidal gold strips run on the same sera found 11 positives, against 34 by commercial ELISA (DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.2c01286). The same particles assembled on Fe3O4 (read in full) gave a visual limit of 10^-8 mg/mL for antibodies to the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein; the paper has no gold-strip comparator in the same study, and in 98 vaccinated volunteers the visible T line identified 49 strong positives, while 18 weak positives were found only by Raman (DOI: 10.1007/s00216-022-04437-1). Au@Ag particles gave a colorimetric LOD of 300 pg/mL for the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein, against 2.16 pg/mL in the SERS channel that is not counted, with negative accuracy 100% and positive accuracy 85% on 20 patients and 20 healthy subjects (DOI: 10.1016/j.aca.2025.344149). Core-shell Au@MBA@Ag particles gave visual LODs of 6.25 x 10^2 TCID50/mL for porcine epidemic diarrhea virus and 7.42 x 10^2 copies/uL for porcine rotavirus A in faecal samples, with 93.3% compliance with RT-PCR (DOI: 10.1016/j.saa.2025.125863). Integrating polydopamine into Au@Ag (read in full) gave a colour channel that was twofold better than the same strip with plain Au@Ag tags (colour disappeared below 0.5 ng/mL for H1N1 and 0.05 ng/mL for the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein) and, for inactivated virus, visual limits of 3.3 x 10^5 copies/mL (H1N1) and 1.23 x 10^5 copies/mL (SARS-CoV-2), 5-fold and 2-fold better than commercial gold strips. The "2 to more than 750 times" of the abstract mixes these colour gains with the SERS gains (up to 756-fold, not counted). Of 32 positive and 4 negative nasopharyngeal swabs verified by qRT-PCR, the colour band was visible in most positives, and four weak positives (H10, S6, S13, S14) were seen only by SERS (DOI: 10.1007/s00604-025-07587-y).

**Gold combined with other materials.** The strategy is to add a second function (magnetic separation, a porous carrier, a high-loading core) while the gold provides the colour. Fe3O4-Au heterodimers gave better coloration than ordinary gold nanoparticles in a strip that read the nucleic acids of influenza H1N1, H3N2 and H9N2 on three test lines (DOI: 10.1039/d4ay01010h); Janus Au(shell)-Fe3O4 particles gave naked-eye LODs of 50 ng/mL for both H3N2 influenza and SARS-CoV-2 in a multiplex strip, against 2 and 7 pg/mL for the photothermal channel that is not counted (DOI: 10.1016/j.bios.2024.117030); and Fe3O4-Au(shell) heterodimers for mpox virus gave a 5 ng/mL naked-eye threshold, against 0.04 ng/mL for photothermal readout (DOI: 10.1016/j.talanta.2025.128670). High-density gold nanoshells on Fe3O4 nanoclusters (MagAu(shell)) added magnetic enrichment before the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid strip, which increased the detection sensitivity by 2 orders (DOI: 10.1016/j.bios.2023.115688). A Fe3O4/Au/quantum-dot probe modified with wheat germ agglutinin captured viral membrane proteins broadly and gave a visual sensitivity of 0.1 to 0.5 ng/mL for SARS-CoV-2 and mpox virus on 33 clinical samples (DOI: 10.1002/smll.202406053). Silica cores carrying a layer of gold and quantum dots (read in full) were used for SARS-CoV-2 IgM and IgG; the paper states that the colorimetric ability of the label is equivalent to a traditional gold LFIA (purple bands visible at a 1:10^4 serum dilution), and the 100-fold gain belongs to the fluorescent channel (1:10^6), which is not counted. In 8 early-stage sera the colour bands were hard to see (only the M line of P2-P3 and the G line of P3-P7 were clear), and the reported sensitivity and specificity of 100% on 16 positive and 41 negative sera rest on fluorescence cut-offs (DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.0c03484); for the SARS-CoV-2 S and N proteins, where the colorimetric limit was 50 pg/mL, 5 times better than a standard AuNP strip, against 2.2 pg/mL by fluorescence, 113 times better (DOI: 10.1021/acsami.2c21042); and for the mpox virus A29L antigen, where the colorimetric limit of 0.5 ng/mL equalled that of the conventional AuNP strip while the fluorescent mode reached 0.0021 ng/mL (DOI: 10.1186/s12951-023-02215-4). Layers of 30 nm AuNPs on MoS2 nanosheets (read in full) gave a visual limit of 0.2 ng/mL for the mpox virus A29L antigen against 1 ng/mL for a standard AuNP strip in the same study, a 5-fold colour gain; the "over 500 times" of the abstract is the SERS limit (0.002 ng/mL) compared with the visual limit of the AuNP strip, and a MoS2@Au tag without the second gold layer had similar visual ability. Real samples were throat swabs from healthy volunteers and lake water spiked with the antigen, not patient samples (DOI: 10.1016/j.jhazmat.2023.132136). Rhodamine B-loaded UiO-66 with AuNPs on the outside gave a colorimetric LOD of 31.2 pg/mL against 462.4 pg/mL for a conventional AuNP strip, about 15-fold (calculated), with 7.6 pg/mL by fluorescence (DOI: 10.3389/fmicb.2026.1876426). Dual-signal readout nanospheres (RNs@Au) detected Ebola virus glycoprotein by naked eye at 2 ng/mL in 20 minutes, with a quantitative limit of 0.18 ng/mL (DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.7b02222). A PDA@Au core-shell nanoprobe for influenza A (H1N1) gave visual sensitivity of 93.9% and specificity 95.8% on 90 throat swabs, with the SERS and photothermal channels that are not counted at 98.5% and 97.0% (DOI: 10.1007/s00604-025-07611-1).

**Surface stabilisation.** Tris-stabilised AuNPs allowed simultaneous visual detection of porcine epidemic diarrhea virus (1.25 x 10^3 TCID50/mL) and porcine rotavirus (3.13 x 10^2 pg/mL), with concordance with RT-PCR of 95% and 100% on clinical samples (DOI: 10.1016/j.saa.2024.124670).

Limit addressed in 3.1.4: the optical effect of one captured particle, and in some designs the number of particles reaching the line (magnetic enrichment). What the colour channels show is modest: where a baseline is stated, the gains are 2- to 5-fold (polydopamine-integrated Au@Ag, MoS2@Au-Au, SiO2/AuNP/QD), 7-fold (nanocages) and about 15-fold (RhB/AuNP UiO-66), one nanostar strip is stated to be two orders below commercial strips, and two designs give no colour gain at all (mpox SiO2-Au/DQD; SARS-CoV-2 SiO2@Au@QD). In the five papers read in full, the headline figure of the abstract belongs to the second channel (SERS or fluorescence) in each case. Cost: multi-step synthesis of the particle, and an instrument for the second channel, which is where most of the sensitivity of these labels lies.

## 3.1.5 Replacing the binder and choosing the antigen

A brighter label cannot help if the binder is weak. A parallel line of work therefore replaces the conventional antibody with a binder that is smaller, more stable or easier to produce.

Nanobodies are small single-domain fragments. In a SARS-CoV-2 spike S1 strip, a nanobody-gold detector with an ACE2-gold capture probe lowered the LOD compared with a monoclonal-antibody version of the same strip, and gave higher sensitivity at low viral loads in saliva (DOI: 10.1038/s41598-023-37347-y). The abstract and the results text disagree on the comparator's sensitivity, so this comparison needs checking against the source tables (DOI: 10.1038/s41598-023-37347-y). Other nanobody designs, including multivalent and Y-shaped assemblies, have been reported for the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid and spike S1 proteins and for the chikungunya virus E protein, and a nanobody strip has been used on human norovirus outbreak specimens (DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.4c00509; DOI: 10.1039/d4nr02762k; DOI: 10.1039/d5nr02568k; DOI: 10.3390/bios16020132; DOI: 10.3389/fimmu.2025.1707358; DOI: 10.1128/msphere.00219-16).

Aptamers are chemically synthesised and can be produced more reproducibly and cheaply than antibodies (DOI: 10.3390/molecules29081776). They can be used in sandwich or competitive formats. A competitive aptamer strip for SARS-CoV-2 spike protein on food packaging used an aptamer chosen for broad binding to variant spike proteins, but its sensitivity was lower than other methods and it was tested only with recombinant protein (DOI: 10.3390/molecules29081776). Other aptamer strips for SARS-CoV-2 (spike protein, inactivated virus and nucleocapsid protein) were evaluated with inactivated virus or spiked samples (DOI: 10.1002/anie.202107730; DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.2c00554; DOI: 10.1186/s12951-023-02191-9).

Further binders include sialic-acid glyconanoparticles that recognise spike through glycans, a lectin for severe dengue virus infection, and a miniprotein binder aimed at SARS-CoV-2 variants of concern (DOI: 10.1021/acscentsci.0c00855; DOI: 10.1080/15321819.2023.2260480; DOI: 10.1007/s00604-023-06113-2). Their abstracts give no accuracy data (Table A1d).

In principle, binder replacement is promising for supply independence and for coping with sequence variation. The evidence so far is mostly analytical, and the clearest like-for-like comparison is the nanobody strip.

Two further parts of the strip decide what the particle can capture: the pair of antibodies, and the antigen used in serology strips. For SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid, two antibody pairs (NP-mAb-7 and -40) reached 8 pg of nucleocapsid protein and 625 TCID50/mL of virus on 60 clinical samples (DOI: 10.3390/ijms222212412), and a detection antibody aimed at a linear epitope reached 10 pg/mL (DOI: 10.3390/ijms25084436). Comparing nucleocapsid and spike as targets for strips was the subject of one study (DOI: 10.1016/j.molimm.2021.01.005). In serology strips the antigen can be the variable: an acid-treated p72 antigen on gold detected African swine fever virus antibodies at a 1:10,000 serum dilution (DOI: 10.3389/fchem.2021.804981); the protrusion domain of the infectious bursal disease virus VP2 protein was used as a highly immunogenic diagnostic antigen (DOI: 10.1007/s00253-020-10458-6); plant-produced receptor-binding domain gave sensitivity 94.1% and specificity 98% on 51 sera (DOI: 10.3877/cma.j.issn.2096-112x.2021.01.006); and baculovirus-expressed nucleocapsid and receptor-binding domain were tested on 103 convalescent sera (DOI: 10.1007/s10529-022-03316-0). The shell domain of norovirus was chosen as the antigen for a broad-range strip tested on 122 clinical samples (DOI: 10.1186/s12866-020-02084-z).

Aptamers and synthetic binders continue the theme. A pair of sandwich aptamers detected whole avian influenza H5N2 particles with enhanced specificity over other H5Nx viruses (DOI: 10.1016/j.bios.2019.03.061), an aptamer system for H3N2 selected by virus-based SELEX is a scope check, because the abstract describes no strip data (DOI: 10.1166/jbn.2019.2789), an aptamer-based isolation step was combined with a strip for grouper nervous necrosis virus (5 ng/mL coat protein) (DOI: 10.3389/fmicb.2020.00886), a nanogold-and-aptamer proof of concept was proposed for at-home detection of SARS-CoV-2 mutants (DOI: 10.1016/j.nano.2022.102590), and a DNA aptamer for the Omicron receptor-binding domain was developed for multiplexed detection and pseudovirus neutralisation, again a scope check (DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.2c01993). A hydrogel nanoparticle acting as an abiotic antibody mimic on a gold core was developed for SARS-CoV-2 spike detection (DOI: 10.1016/j.talanta.2025.129324).

In every case in this group the abstract gives either a detection limit or a specificity statement, not a like-for-like comparison with an antibody version of the same strip. The exception is the nanobody strip above (DOI: 10.1038/s41598-023-37347-y).

## 3.1.6 Assay format: what the line is able to show

Even with a good label, the format of the immunoassay limits what colour can mean. In a sandwich strip, too much antigen can suppress the line (the hook effect). A study of lumpy skin disease virus investigated this effect directly, reporting a detection limit of 10^3.4 TCID50/mL, a coefficient of variation below 5.3% and no cross-reaction with 12 viruses (DOI: 10.3390/bios12090739). In competitive formats the line gets weaker as the target increases, which suits small analytes and functional assays: a competitive gold strip for SARS-CoV-2 neutralising antibodies gave 20.66 IU/L with 100% specificity (DOI: 10.1021/acsomega.2c03677), and a biomimetic mock-virus particle with trimeric SARS-CoV-2 spike measured neutralising antibody at 59 IU/mL, benchmarked to the WHO standard (DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.3c04372). Quantitative comparison of kits is another use of the format: a quantitative colloidal-gold assay measured the detection limit of nine approved SARS-CoV-2 antigen kits against the nucleocapsid protein and found nucleic-acid testing at least 1835 times more sensitive than antigen testing on a Delta strain (DOI: 10.1016/j.talanta.2023.124462). A Japanese encephalitis virus NS1 strip with a smartphone colorimeter reached 10 pg/mL by eye in spiked serum and was stable for one month at 4 C (DOI: 10.1039/d2na00463a).

The limit addressed here is interpretation: whether the colour on the line has a one-to-one relation with the amount of target. The cost is that each format has a different dynamic range, and most of these abstracts do not state it.

## 3.1.7 Changing the optics: plasmonic scattering as a boundary case
All the strategies above increase absorbed colour. One study instead removes the white background that hides a different optical signal. When the nitrocellulose membrane is made transparent by refractive-index matching and placed over a black backing card, large AuNPs appear as orange lines by scattering rather than absorption, and the background almost disappears; the study targeted influenza A virus nucleoprotein (DOI: 10.1038/s41467-025-58663-z). The authors report a very large gain against their own conventional strip and against commercial strips, a higher sensitivity on a small set of clinical swabs, and a naked-eye or smartphone readout (DOI: 10.1038/s41467-025-58663-z). The approach needs an extra reagent step and sufficient light, and the authors place it beyond absorbance-based colorimetric detection. We therefore keep it as a flagged boundary case and report its figures separately from absorption-based strips (Table A1c).

## 3.1.8 Conventional gold strips without engineering

Many included records describe new colloidal-gold strips that apply none of the steps above. They serve as baseline: they show what the unmodified format achieves, and they are the strips that the engineering papers set out to improve. Their abstracts rarely give a within-paper change, so they are summarised here by target, with values in Table A1f.

For SARS-CoV-2 antibodies, IgM and IgG strips reported sensitivity and specificity against patient sera: sensitivity 100% and specificity 93.3% (kappa 0.872) for IgM (DOI: 10.1021/acsomega.0c01554), sensitivity 90% and specificity 96.6% against real-time PCR on 60 samples (DOI: 10.1089/mab.2021.0027), and others without values in the abstract (DOI: 10.1039/d0an00629g; DOI: 10.1097/cm9.0000000000000922); the colloidal-gold assays in Table A1e add 158/170 positives with AUC 0.958 (DOI: 10.1002/jcla.23619) and IgM sensitivity of 90.67% (DOI: 10.1002/ctm2.90). A nasal secretory IgA strip detected spike-specific IgA purified from people infected with BA.5 (DOI: 10.3389/fmicb.2024.1386891). For SARS-CoV-2 antigen, a spike strip detected 62.5 ng/mL in a subunit vaccine (DOI: 10.3389/fimmu.2021.635677), a nucleocapsid kit more than 0.2 ng/mL recombinant protein without cross-reaction (DOI: 10.1016/j.jviromet.2021.114183), a gold-nanoparticle receptor-binding-domain strip detected 1 ng/mL with sensitivity 94.3% and specificity 90.9% against RT-PCR on 100 clinical samples (DOI: 10.1002/jmv.28416), a nanogold test reached sensitivity 95% and specificity 98% on clinical specimens (DOI: 10.3390/nano12142477), another detected 2 ng/mL with 98% concordance (DOI: 10.1007/s00604-025-07299-3), and the CoV-SCAN strip detected 10 variants at 1.6-12.5 TCID50/swab (DOI: 10.1016/j.jcvp.2022.100080). A nucleic-acid sandwich strip for the N3 gene reached 5 pM (DOI: 10.1016/j.jbiosc.2022.11.001).

For other human viruses: an HIV-1 p24 strip with in-house monoclonal antibodies reached 25 pg/mL and detected 10/10 positives with specificity 98.03% (DOI: 10.1155/2016/6743904); an SFTSV strip reached 1 ng/mL (DOI: 10.1021/acsomega.8b02366); a feline coronavirus antibody strip was tested on plasma, serum, whole blood and ascitic fluid (DOI: 10.1007/978-1-4939-2438-7_3); a dengue competitive strip with 4G2 reached 5.12 x 10^2 PFU (DOI: 10.3390/bios12070495); a varicella-zoster strip reached 30 ng/mL gE without cross-reactivity with EV71, HSV-1 or HSV-2 (DOI: 10.1016/j.virol.2023.07.008); and an HTLV-I IgG strip showed sensitivity 96.7% and specificity 100% (DOI: 10.3390/v15010129). For animal and aquatic viruses: a nervous necrosis virus strip for whole virions on fish tissue (DOI: 10.1038/s41598-020-63553-z), porcine circovirus 2 at 10^3.18 TCID50/mL (DOI: 10.1128/spectrum.01953-22), pseudorabies virus at 1.336 x 10^3 copies/uL (DOI: 10.3389/fmicb.2024.1399123), a canine parvovirus strip on faecal samples (sensitivity 71%, specificity 92% against haemagglutination) (DOI: 10.1007/s00705-018-3846-2), canine parvovirus strips, one in colloidal gold and fluorescent format on 60 clinical samples and one in nanogold with sensitivity 96.4% and specificity 88.2% against qPCR (DOI: 10.1007/s00253-023-12604-2; DOI: 10.1038/s41598-024-55548-x).

These records show how widely the format has been adapted, but they say little about signal improvement, because no engineered comparison strip is run beside them. A strip is listed in this group when its abstract describes no deliberate change to the label, binder or readout.

## 3.1.9 What the evidence supports

For the question of this review, the studies of this part give a consistent picture. The colour of a gold strip is made stronger in three ways: by making the captured gold more visible after capture (enlargement, layer assembly, an amplifier complex), by a better particle (clusters, dimers, shells, shaped gold), and, in a few designs, by delivering more particles to the line (magnetic enrichment). The best-supported comparisons are those in which the same paper runs its own conventional strip (Table A4). Within those papers the reported gain in the colour signal ranges from about 2-fold to about 1000-fold; the high end belongs to designs with extra hardware or reagents (a magnet, an electrode set, an imager), and shaped or composite gold labels give gains of about 5- to 15-fold in the colour channel. A second finding is that the structured gold labels of 3.1.4 owe most of their headline sensitivity to a second signal, so the gain in colour alone is much smaller than the title of such a paper suggests; in the five of them read in full, the colour gain was 2- to 5-fold or absent, and the figure quoted in the abstract belonged to SERS or fluorescence. Conjugation chemistry, binder replacement, antigen design and assay format address other limits (activity of the antibody, supply, variants, interpretation of the line); their abstracts seldom state a change in the colour signal against a baseline.

Three limitations apply across the part. First, many reported LODs come from scanners, imagers or readers rather than the naked eye, so they describe instrument-assisted performance. Second, clinical evaluation is the exception among engineering papers: several tested only spiked buffer, saliva or recombinant protein, and the reference method varies (ELISA, RT-PCR, gel-read RT-PCR). Third, comparators differ (own conventional strip, same-size particles, commercial strip), so fold-changes cannot be ranked. Reporting quality of the papers read in full is in Table A3. Two animal-virus strips (DOI: 10.1039/d4an00180j; DOI: 10.1039/d2an02043b) are still known from abstracts only. Eighteen of the 119 papers have been read in full, in some cases in the main text only.

### Table A2. Strategy overview for gold-nanoparticle engineering

| Strategy | Bottleneck addressed | Approaches in the included studies | Reported within-paper gains (see tables for values) | Costs and limits seen in the evidence | Table |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Size and surface tuning | antibody activity, particle mobility | size series, oriented conjugation, additives, corona and aggregation studies | less antibody needed with oriented conjugation; serotype-specific LODs with an 80 nm particle | mostly mechanistic; few diagnostic data | A1a |
| Enlargement and amplification | size of the captured signal | silver, gold and galvanic enhancement, self-enhancement, layer assembly, amplifier complex | LOD lowered within the same strip format, by up to more than two orders of magnitude | extra steps, background, lowest values often need calibration or a reader | A1b |
| Structured particles | optical effect per particle | clusters, dimers, nanoshells, composites, nanostars | about 4- to 24-fold against the authors' own strips; 50-fold against a commercial strip (dimers) | scale-up (dimers), buffer-only tests (nanostars), instrument imaging (two-wavelength) | A1c |
| Binder replacement and antigen design | binder quality, supply, variants | nanobodies, aptamers, glycans, lectins, miniproteins, antibody pairs, recombinant and plant-made antigens | nanobody: lower LOD than mAb in the same strip | aptamer strips less sensitive; mostly spiked or inactivated-virus samples | A1d |
| Optical readout change | background of the white membrane | plasmonic scattering on a black backing | one study: 361-fold against its own strip, sensitivity 90% on 30 positive swabs | extra reagent step, light source, boundary to colorimetric | A1c |

# Part B (working text v2): other coloured labels and the colorimetric signal

Working text, 2026-11-10. Reframed around the question of this review: how does each non-gold coloured label change the colour signal of a strip, by how much, and at what cost? Each subsection follows four questions: what limits the signal, which label addresses it, what the papers report against their own baseline (usually a gold strip or a fluorescent label), and what the change costs. The target virus is named for every method. Numbers are in the tables; Table B4 gathers the within-paper comparisons. † = read in full; all other rows abstract only (NR = not reported in the abstract). Every DOI cited in the text appears in a table and in the evidence table. The section covers 33 included records (R07568 excluded as not a conventional LFA; pre-2015 records removed), all from 2015-2026; seven were read in full.

# 3.2 Other coloured labels: what a different colour carrier changes

## 3.2.0 What limits the signal of a gold strip, and how this section is organised

Three limits of the gold label are given in the papers of this section. The first is optics: polydopamine nanoparticles absorb over a broader and stronger band than gold, which the authors of a SARS-CoV-2 IgM/IgG strip used as the reason for the change (DOI: 10.3390/bios13030352). The second is manufacturing: colloidal gold strips are described as limited in analytical sensitivity and affected by batch-to-batch differences in particle size, while dyed latex beads are bright and uniform in size and can be coupled to antibodies in a controlled way, as argued for porcine epidemic diarrhea virus and porcine circovirus 2 strips (DOI: 10.1186/s44149-021-00029-1; DOI: 10.1007/s00253-022-12074-y). The third is information: particles of different colours let one strip report more than one analyte, as in a strip for African swine fever virus and classical swine fever virus antibodies (DOI: 10.1155/tbed/5512419).

The section follows the labels from the most developed to the least. Coloured latex microspheres come first (3.2.1), then colour coding for multiplexing (3.2.2), carbon (3.2.3), selenium, silver and other inorganic labels (3.2.4), organic and polymer chromophores (3.2.5) and cellulose nanobeads (3.2.6). Section 3.2.7 collects the studies in which a coloured label was less sensitive than a fluorescent one, and 3.2.8 states what the evidence supports.

**How the signal is read.** Reading the abstracts of the 33 records in this section, 18 state that the strip is read by eye, 0 state that a device is used (smartphone, reader, scanner or imager), 3 state either eye or device, 12 describe a strip without saying how it is read, and 0 has no abstract. 4 records report a second readout or a non-colour comparator (fluorescence, SERS, thermal, electrochemical); they are marked ‡ in the tables, and only the colorimetric channel is counted as a result of this review. "Not stated" means only that the abstract is silent. Records read in full give their reading mode from the full text (marked "full text" in the tables); the counts above refer to abstracts.

## 3.2.1 Coloured latex microspheres

Latex microspheres are polymer particles a few hundred nanometres in diameter that are dyed in a chosen colour and carry carboxyl groups for covalent coupling to antibodies (DOI: 10.1186/s44149-021-00029-1; DOI: 10.1155/tbed/5512419). Their size is a design constraint as well as an advantage. Large particles aggregate easily, so the amount of protein coupled to the surface has to be set at the highest level that does not cause sedimentation (DOI: 10.1155/tbed/5512419).

Most of the latex work in this review concerns animal viruses detected directly as antigen, and two studies read in full show what such strips can and cannot do. A red-latex strip for porcine circovirus 2 used monoclonal antibodies raised against the dominant genotypes and detected the three main genotypes, but was clearly less sensitive for the older PCV2a genotype than for PCV2b and PCV2d, which the authors attribute to the immunogens (DOI: 10.1007/s00253-022-12074-y). Against PCR on tissue and swab samples it was fully specific but missed some PCR-positive samples. The authors explain this partly by the fact that PCR detects viral DNA whether or not it is infectious virus (DOI: 10.1007/s00253-022-12074-y). A latex strip for porcine epidemic diarrhea virus in faeces solved a practical problem of the sample itself: solid faecal particles disturbed the strip, and a filter pad placed between the sample pad and the conjugate pad improved agreement with RT-PCR (DOI: 10.1186/s44149-021-00029-1). Both studies therefore illustrate that a coloured carrier alone does not set performance; antibody choice and sample handling matter as much.

Latex strips have also been made for SARS-CoV-2 antigen (25 ng/mL in positive specimens), for North American porcine reproductive and respiratory syndrome virus (PRRSV; sensitivity 90.32% on clinical samples) and, with a sialylglycopolymer-latex probe, for the receptor specificity of influenza A group 1 viruses, where the strip correlated above 0.95 with an ELISA (DOI: 10.1002/jcla.24091; DOI: 10.3389/fmicb.2022.882112; DOI: 10.1016/j.bios.2014.10.036). A latex double-antigen sandwich for SARS-CoV-2 neutralizing antibodies had a LOD of 7.11 ng/mL against 1.13 ng/mL for the fluorescent-bead version, with sensitivity 98.29% and specificity 97.70% against a virus neutralization test (DOI: 10.1016/j.talanta.2022.124200). A latex strip for the SARS-CoV-2 N protein detected 10 ng/mL, against 15 pg/mL for a sandwich ELISA with the same antibody pair in the same study (DOI: 10.1111/1751-7915.70117). Composite carriers combine latex with a second material. Gold or platinum nanoparticle-latex nanocomposites were used for influenza virus, with enhanced sensitivity whose value the abstract does not give (DOI: 10.1021/acsami.8b11745); gold-coated polystyrene latex for influenza H3 reached 1/16 HAU by eye and 0.016 HAU quantitatively (DOI: 10.1007/s00604-020-04255-1); highly chromatic red silica nanoparticles doped with Direct Red 224 detected H7 avian influenza (H7N2 and H7N9) at 0.08 ng/mL (DOI: 10.1039/d1ay00204j); and dye-loaded polymersomes served as the carrier of a SARS-CoV-2 colorimetric strip (DOI: 10.1021/acssensors.1c00854). Their abstracts report mainly analytical data (Table B1a). The limit addressed in 3.2.1 is uniformity and brightness of the carrier; the cost is that sensitivity is set by the antibody pair and sample handling as much as by the colour, and the SARS-CoV-2 N-protein result above shows that a latex strip can be far less sensitive than a laboratory ELISA.

## 3.2.2 Colour as a code: multicolour strips for multiplexing

Because coloured carriers come in distinct hues, one strip can report more than one analyte and the result is read by colour. A strip with red, blue and black latex gives a red line for African swine fever virus (ASFV) antibodies, a blue line for classical swine fever virus (CSFV) antibodies, and a black control line (DOI: 10.1155/tbed/5512419). Its visual detection limit was equal to a commercial ELISA for ASFV and better than the commercial ELISA for CSFV, and agreement with the ELISAs on 180 sera was high for both (DOI: 10.1155/tbed/5512419). The authors point out a limit that no label can remove: CSFV E2 antibodies cannot separate vaccinated from infected animals, so the CSFV line mainly serves to evaluate vaccine response (DOI: 10.1155/tbed/5512419).

Other multicolour designs use a bicolour latex strip for SARS-CoV-2 neutralizing antibodies (13 serum samples, about 48 ng/mL) and a red/blue strip that reads a duplex RT-LAMP for human norovirus GII (5.8 copies/uL) and human adenovirus F41 (63 copies/uL) (DOI: 10.3390/bios12020103; DOI: 10.3389/fmicb.2026.1787049). A further design uses colour only to make the result easier to read: a red latex test line for ASFV antibodies and a blue latex control line made with chicken IgY, which read by eye within 15 min, agreed closely with a commercial ELISA on 159 sera and tolerated months of storage at 4 C, room temperature and 37 C (DOI: 10.1007/s00253-025-13694-w). In that study the authors found that larger particles (400 nm) gave a weak false-positive line, and settled on 300 nm (DOI: 10.1007/s00253-025-13694-w). Colour coding keeps the strip equipment-free, but it relies on the user telling hues apart, and none of these abstracts reports blinded or multi-reader reading (Table B1b).

## 3.2.3 Carbon-based labels

Carbon labels give a dark signal from a material very different from the metals. Carbon black was used as the label for a severe fever with thrombocytopenia syndrome virus (SFTSV) strip with a detection limit of 100 pg/mL in spiked serum, and in a vertical-flow format for SARS-CoV-2 IgG, where the authors report a lower LOD than colloidal gold without giving the value (DOI: 10.1039/d3an00515a; DOI: 10.3390/bios13090857). The only head-to-head comparison with gold and clinical samples, for porcine deltacoronavirus (PDCoV) on 137 faecal samples, found the carbon strip less sensitive than the gold strip (1:64 against 1:128 at 10^8 TCID50/mL; agreement above 88% against 92.7%) (DOI: 10.1016/j.isci.2026.116948). The limit addressed is cost and the dark, high-contrast signal; the cost is that sensitivity was not improved in the one direct comparison (Table B1c).

## 3.2.4 Selenium, silver, ultramarine blue and magnetic labels

Several inorganic particles have been proposed as red or blue labels. Selenium nanoparticles were used for SARS-CoV-2 antibodies (IgM 20 ng/mL, IgG 5 ng/mL; sensitivity 93.33% and specificity 97.34% on 90 patients and 263 controls) and, with structured protein probes on a chip, for SARS-CoV-2 antigen in throat swab, saliva, serum and finger-prick blood (10 pg/mL; overall coincidence 95.83% with RT-PCR in 192 samples) (DOI: 10.1039/d0lc00828a; DOI: 10.1016/j.bios.2024.116878); a second selenium antibody strip gives no values in its abstract (DOI: 10.1109/tnb.2021.3105662). Silver is a stronger absorber than gold but is unstable; coating the particles with a thin calixarene layer made them stable in plasma and for months of storage, and carboxyl groups on the coating allowed covalent coupling of the SARS-CoV-2 spike protein (DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.2c00870). The particles give a yellow line, not a red one, and in a dipstick assay for anti-SARS-CoV-2 IgG they gave a lower LOD and similar signals with ten times fewer particles than gold particles with adsorbed protein (DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.2c00870). The gain was shown in buffer; in human plasma the LOD was several hundred-fold higher, and on 25 serum samples the dipstick was fully specific but detected only about three-quarters of the PCR-positive patients, which the authors say matches typical gold-based strips (DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.2c00870). Ultramarine blue, an inexpensive inorganic pigment, was used as a blue label for the hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg), with a LOD of 0.37 ng/mL, and read by eye or by image analysis; it was tested in spiked fetal calf serum and not in patient samples. The abstract states a lower LOD than a gold strip, whereas the main-text comparisons found are with literature values for iron-oxide and red-silica labels, and a quantum-dot method is described as more sensitive (DOI: 10.1016/j.aca.2019.11.037). Magnetic oxide labels combine colour with a magnetic core: a natural-colour Fe3O4 strip for SARS-CoV-2 reached 0.5 ng/mL (DOI: 10.1016/j.ijbiomac.2026.150960). All of these are known from abstracts only. A head-to-head gold comparator is reported for the silver and ultramarine-blue strips (Table B1d).

## 3.2.5 Organic and polymer chromophores

Polydopamine (PDA) nanoparticles show how a new label needs surface engineering as much as gold does. PDA sticks strongly to nitrocellulose, and probes coupled directly to it would be trapped at the test line and give false negatives; the authors therefore coated the particles with polyelectrolyte layers, silica and PEG-silane before coupling antigen and antibody (DOI: 10.3390/bios13030352). The resulting three-line strip separated IgG and IgM and was quantified with a camera and image software. In the same study a commercial gold strip barely detected low antibody concentrations (DOI: 10.3390/bios13030352). The study used serum spiked with standard antibodies, not patient samples. Related labels include black polylevodopa nanoparticles for SARS-CoV-2 (5 pg/mL) and influenza A H1N1 (0.1 ng/mL), (DOI: 10.1016/j.bbrc.2024.149821), and polydiacetylene vesicles that show a red line and red fluorescence at the same time, used for HBsAg: the red line was confirmed by naked eye up to 1 ng/mL, whereas the fluorescent image reached 0.1 ng/mL, so only the 1 ng/mL figure is a colorimetric result (DOI: 10.1002/asia.201700769). The abstracts give little accuracy data (Table B1e).

## 3.2.6 Cellulose nanobeads

Cellulose nanobeads are dyed cellulose particles. They were used for SARS-CoV-2 antigen with an scFv-Fc binder (2 ng antigen; 2.5 x 10^4 pfu of cultured virus) (DOI: 10.1016/j.bios.2020.112868), for a comparison with a 40 nm gold strip for the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid against the WHO target product profile, where the cellulose cut-off was 100 times below the 10^6 copies/mL profile threshold and the gold strip met it (DOI: 10.1039/d5ra08197a), and for Nipah virus nucleoprotein in inactivated cultures, with 1 ng/mL nucleoprotein, 4.1 x 10^7 copies/mL by reader and 8.3 x 10^7 by naked eye (DOI: 10.1080/22221751.2026.2716499). They are the most recent group here and the evidence is limited to analytical data (Table B1f).

## 3.2.7 Where coloured labels fall short

Several studies compare a coloured label with a fluorescent label and show the cost of equipment-free reading. Fluorescent microspheres were more sensitive than coloured microspheres in strips for rotavirus and adenovirus and for influenza A and B (DOI: 10.1186/s12985-018-0951-5; DOI: 10.1186/s12985-019-1190-0). In a SARS-CoV-2 neutralizing-antibody strip the fluorescent version reached a lower LOD than the latex colorimetric version (DOI: 10.1016/j.talanta.2022.124200). For antibodies against the human adenovirus 3 hexon protein, quantum dots reached the higher positive agreement of the two labels tested (100% against 96.9% for gold) (DOI: 10.3390/diagnostics16162670). These results are consistent with a trade-off, in which coloured labels give up some sensitivity in exchange for reading without a fluorescence reader (Table B1g).

## 3.2.8 What the evidence supports

For the question of this review, the answer is that non-gold coloured labels mainly change what the strip can do, not how strong the colour is. Coloured latex is the best-documented alternative to gold, mainly in veterinary virology (porcine epidemic diarrhea virus, porcine circovirus 2, PRRSV, African and classical swine fever virus), and multicolour latex lets several analytes share a test region and be told apart by colour. Where a comparison with gold exists in the same paper, the reported gain in the colour signal is small or absent: silver was one order of magnitude lower in LOD in buffer for SARS-CoV-2 IgG but several hundred-fold worse in plasma; polydopamine quantified low SARS-CoV-2 antibody concentrations better than a commercial gold strip; cellulose nanobeads reached a lower cut-off than gold for the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid; and carbon was less sensitive than gold for porcine deltacoronavirus (Table B4). Against fluorescent labels the coloured labels were consistently less sensitive. The other labels rest largely on single studies and abstracts.

Three limitations recur. First, reference methods differ between studies (PCR, RT-PCR, commercial ELISA, virus neutralization), so agreement values cannot be compared across them. Second, several studies used spiked or standard samples only. Third, genotype or strain coverage depends on the antibody pair and not on the label. For the seven papers read in full, Table B3 lists the reporting items. Two key papers for this section (carbon black and selenium) remain at abstract level until their PDFs are available.

# Part C (working text v2): catalytic and enzymatic amplification and the colorimetric signal

Working text, 2026-11-10. Reframed around the question of this review: how does catalytic amplification change the colour signal of a strip, by how much, and at what cost? Each subsection follows four questions: what limits the signal, which amplifier addresses it, what the papers report against their own baseline, and what it costs. The target virus is named for every method. Numbers are in the tables; Table C4 gathers the within-paper changes in the colour signal. † = read in full; all other rows abstract only (NR = not reported in the abstract). Every DOI cited in the text appears in a table and in the evidence table. The section covers 48 included records after the E10 exclusion of seven marketed-kit evaluations and the removal of pre-2015 records, all from 2015-2026; six were read in full.

# 3.3 Catalytic and enzymatic amplification: adding colour after capture

## 3.3.0 What limits the colour, and how this section is organised

The colour of a conventional strip is limited by what the label itself absorbs, and by how many labels the antibodies manage to capture (DOI: 10.1021/acsnano.7b06229). Amplification adds a second source of colour: after the sandwich has formed, something at the test line produces or deposits more of it. Enzyme-catalysed amplification is standard in laboratory immunoassays, but conventional implementations need technical skill and equipment that suit field use poorly (DOI: 10.1021/acsnano.7b06229). The studies in this section try to bring catalytic amplification into the strip while keeping it simple enough for the point of need.

The approaches can be read as a progression in how much of the amplifier is built into the strip. The first group deposits metal on particles that are already captured (3.3.1). The second uses natural enzymes such as horseradish peroxidase and alkaline phosphatase as labels (3.3.2). The third replaces enzymes with inorganic nanozymes, particles that mimic enzyme activity and are described by their authors as more stable and easier to scale up (3.3.3 and 3.3.4; DOI: 10.1016/j.snb.2021.130718; DOI: 10.3390/plants15040656). The fourth tries to make the amplification step automatic (3.3.5). The last group combines catalytic colour with a second readout, and only the colorimetric channel counts here (3.3.6). Section 3.3.7 states what the evidence supports. Seven evaluations of marketed amplification kits were excluded under the rule agreed for commercial-kit evaluations (E10); they are listed in the supplement.

**How the signal is read.** Reading the abstracts of the 47 records in this section, 23 state that the strip is read by eye, 2 state that a device is used (smartphone, reader, scanner or imager), 3 state either eye or device, 19 describe a strip without saying how it is read, and 0 has no abstract. 5 records report a second readout or a non-colour comparator (fluorescence, SERS, thermal, electrochemical); they are marked ‡ in the tables, and only the colorimetric channel is counted as a result of this review. "Not stated" means only that the abstract is silent. Records read in full give their reading mode from the full text (marked "full text" in the tables); the counts above refer to abstracts.

## 3.3.1 Depositing more metal on captured particles

The simplest amplifier uses the particles themselves as seeds for further metal deposition. Silver enhancement lowered the detection limit of an equine influenza virus strip 5-fold against conventional rapid tests, and of a foot-and-mouth disease virus (FMDV) strip covering seven serotypes 10- to 100-fold against a commercial strip (DOI: 10.1292/jvms.17-0105; DOI: 10.1016/j.jviromet.2019.113736). Gold enhancement lowered the LOD of an infectious spleen and kidney necrosis virus (ISKNV) strip from 0.25 to 0.050 ug/mL, a 5-fold gain (DOI: 10.1016/j.talanta.2026.130562). Copper deposition on gold seeds extended the approach to the HIV-1 p24 antigen (50 fg/mL, in a strip that also detected Escherichia coli O157:H7) and to the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein (a three-order range down to 10 pg/mL) (DOI: 10.1016/j.bios.2020.112753; DOI: 10.3390/bios12010013). A three-dimensional device combines polyA gold nanoparticles with gold and silver deposition in one step for SARS-CoV-2 nucleic acid, but its abstract gives no values (DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.5c03575). A self-enhancing triplex strip (SET-LFIA) placed two enhancement pads loaded with Au(3+) and hydroxylamine beneath the detection layer, so that gold grows in situ after the immunoreaction without an added user step; it reached 1.0 ng/mL for influenza A, 1.0 ng/mL for influenza B and 0.2 ng/mL for SARS-CoV-2, 5- to 25-fold better than conventional LFIA, improved the discrimination of weak-positive specimens and kept more than 85% of its signal after 21 days at 45 C (DOI: 10.1016/j.talanta.2025.129228). These designs follow the logic described in section 3.1.2: the gain is large, but each needs an extra reagent step and carries a risk of background. One related approach redesigned the gold-nanosphere strip for SARS-CoV-2 and added thermal contrast amplification: the abstract states that the strip detects the spike receptor-binding domain visually down to 3.6 aM in buffer and 28.6 aM in nasopharyngeal wash, and that a thermal-contrast reading step (under 1 minute) distinguished 26-32% of the visual false negatives of commercial strips; the visual figures are colorimetric, the thermal reading is not (DOI: 10.1021/acsanm.1c03217). The limit addressed in 3.3.1 is the size of the captured particle; the cost is an added reagent step and background risk.

## 3.3.2 Natural enzymes as labels

Horseradish peroxidase (HRP) and alkaline phosphatase (ALP) produce a coloured precipitate from a substrate. On a strip they have been used as the sole label, together with gold, or on magnetic carriers. ALP combined with gold nanoparticles lowered the LOD of a potato virus X strip to 0.3 ng/mL, 27-fold below a conventional strip (DOI: 10.1007/s00604-017-2595-3). An HRP-labelled strip for influenza A and B was more than 10-fold more sensitive than gold-colloidal strips on 13 isolates, with sensitivity 90.2% (55/61) and 82.6% (71/86) in nasopharyngeal samples (DOI: 10.1002/jmv.25322), and HRP added to a colloidal-gold strip for bovine parvovirus gave a 10-fold gain over the traditional strip (10^2 TCID50) on faecal samples (DOI: 10.3389/fmicb.2023.1174737). A magnetic enzyme label detected dengue virus NS1 of serotypes 1 to 4 at 0.1 to 1.0 ng/mL with 100% sensitivity and specificity on clinical samples, and a capture-layer design with an enzyme label detected dengue NS1 with 5-fold higher sensitivity than a gold-standard ELISA (DOI: 10.7717/peerj.7779; DOI: 10.1021/acsomega.0c00367). A hepatitis B e-antigen strip used a signal amplification system, with a mechanism not stated in the abstract, and was 27-fold more sensitive than the conventional strip (DOI: 10.1002/jmv.25452). Enzyme-induced tyramide deposition produced a dual-colour amplification for the SARS-CoV-2 nucleoprotein and influenza A/B (3.9 pg/mL; naked-eye cut-off 7.8 pg/mL) (DOI: 10.1016/j.bios.2024.116262), and HRP tracers detected hepatitis B virus DNA in a vertical-flow format with 100% consistency with real-time PCR (DOI: 10.1007/s00216-022-03988-7).

The main weakness of natural enzymes is stability. In one direct test, freeze-dried antibody-HRP conjugates lost most of their signal within two weeks of dry storage at room temperature, whereas a platinum nanocatalyst kept its signal for weeks, even at 44 C (DOI: 10.1021/acsnano.7b06229). That observation is the main reason given for nanozymes in the studies that follow. The limit addressed in 3.3.2 is the number of coloured product molecules per captured label; the cost is a substrate step, and a label that loses activity in dry storage.

## 3.3.3 Noble-metal nanozymes

Nanozymes are particles with peroxidase-like activity; supplied with hydrogen peroxide and a chromogenic substrate, they oxidise it and deposit colour at the test line. Platinum-based particles dominate the evidence.

A porous platinum core-shell nanocatalyst for the HIV-1 p24 antigen (0.8 pg/mL) is the most thoroughly characterised example (DOI: 10.1021/acsnano.7b06229). Its activity depends on particle size, on the polymer capping used in synthesis and on the porous platinum shell, which stays accessible to hydrogen peroxide even when serum proteins coat the surface; the best size range was 120-200 nm, and larger particles performed worse (DOI: 10.1021/acsnano.7b06229). The same strip works in two regimes: at high antigen the intrinsic dark colour of the particle is visible, and at low antigen the catalytic step takes over, which widens the dynamic range over several orders of magnitude (DOI: 10.1021/acsnano.7b06229). The authors tested serial plasma from an HIV seroconversion panel and detected antigen during the acute phase, and lyophilised reagents survived weeks of accelerated ageing (DOI: 10.1021/acsnano.7b06229). The assay needs a wash step and a 5-min development step, so it is not a one-step strip.

Gold-platinum particles follow the same principle. A nanoenzyme-linked sensor with Au@Pt particles for the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein (0.026 ng/mL; positivity 76.2% against 47.6% for ELISA) reads the developed substrate by transmitted light in a small 3D-printed holder with a laser, a photometer and a smartphone, so the result is colorimetric but not equipment-free (DOI: 10.1016/j.snb.2021.130718). The reported LOD was similar to a laboratory ELISA and lower than that of a conventional strip with the same antibodies, and the Au@Pt particles kept their activity over days while HRP lost it (DOI: 10.1016/j.snb.2021.130718). Platinum nanoparticles have also been used to make amplification reagent-free: in a strip for influenza A, a flow-delay pad and an acidic secondary flow deliver TMB and peroxide to the captured particles after the sample, so the user adds nothing but the sample, and a smartphone application reads the intensity ratio (DOI: 10.1021/acsinfecdis.5c00731). Against RT-PCR on 42 clinical samples tested in triplicate the reported sensitivity was 96.8% and specificity 98.4%, but these figures count the 126 replicate measurements, not 42 independent patients (DOI: 10.1021/acsinfecdis.5c00731). Related noble-metal nanozymes include peroxide-tolerant Au@Pt for potato virus X (4 pg/mL in tuber, 8 pg/mL in leaf) (DOI: 10.1016/j.talanta.2020.121961), oxidase-like Au@Pt nanorods for hepatitis B virus DNA that need no hydrogen peroxide (8.5 pM) (DOI: 10.1039/d1an02084f), trimetallic Au@Pd@Pt for the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein (0.037 ng/mL, 17-fold lower than the unenhanced strip) (DOI: 10.1039/d2ay01530g), an AuPt nanoalloy for human papillomavirus 16 (HPV16) DNA (DOI: 10.1039/d3ra00757j), Au@Pt for the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein (40 pg/mL by naked eye, more than 1000-fold against the strip without amplification) and for respiratory syncytial virus F protein (0.78 ng/mL, about 4-fold against colloidal gold) (DOI: 10.1007/s00604-024-06697-3; DOI: 10.1016/j.talanta.2026.130820), Au@Pt@Pd for the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein (16-fold and 8-fold against two other strips; sensitivity 84.09%, specificity 100%) (DOI: 10.1007/s00604-024-06819-x), a platinum nanozyme with a mesoporous silica shell for enrichment, which reached 33 copies/mL for influenza A, about 100-fold against gold strips (DOI: 10.1016/j.aca.2024.343474), Pt-Pd particles for foot-and-mouth disease virus serotype O (sensitivity 96.7% on positive serum) (DOI: 10.1016/j.jchromb.2024.124043), PdRu nanocages for HPV16 and HPV18 (0.93 and 0.19 nM) (DOI: 10.1016/j.jcis.2024.06.002), polydopamine-platinum particles for influenza A (2-fold colorimetric and 200-fold catalytic gain against a gold strip) (DOI: 10.1186/s12951-025-03903-z) and the influenza A platinum strip described above, which reached 11.6 pg/mL, 1000-fold lower than the traditional strip (DOI: 10.1021/acsinfecdis.5c00731). A platinum-polymer nanoparticle strip for SARS-CoV-2 evaluated on 200 clinical specimens had a sensitivity of 74% and a specificity of 100% (DOI: 10.3390/biomedicines13122993), a reminder that amplification does not remove the limits that antibody pairs and sample type set (Table C1c). The limit addressed in 3.3.3 is turnover per label; the cost is peroxide and substrate, a development step, and in the best-characterised case a wash step.

## 3.3.4 Other nanozymes and structured catalysts

Iron-oxide nanozymes were among the earliest: a Fe3O4 nanozyme strip for Ebola virus glycoprotein, developed with DAB and peroxide after the run, reached 1 ng/mL by eye against 100 ng/mL for a standard colloidal-gold strip and 0.1 ng/mL for an ELISA (DOI: 10.1016/j.bios.2015.05.025). Because no Ebola patient samples were available, the authors tested pseudo-virus in serum and, as a surrogate, serum from 20 PCR-positive and 31 negative patients with a different bunyavirus infection; the strip detected 12 of 20 positives, against 8 of 20 for the ELISA, with no false positives (DOI: 10.1016/j.bios.2015.05.025). A study on potato viruses shows how differently nanozymes can be used (DOI: 10.3390/plants15040656). Fe3O4 particles were applied as a plain colorimetric label read by eye within 10 minutes, with no substrate step, to build monovalent and bivalent strips for potato viruses X and Y; the authors note that substrate-based amplification could raise sensitivity further, but did not test it (DOI: 10.3390/plants15040656). All 12 tissue-culture plantlets matched RT-PCR, and the authors state that storage stability and batch consistency were not evaluated, even though the abstract mentions storage at ambient temperature (DOI: 10.3390/plants15040656).

Further designs vary the chemistry. A PDA@MnO2 oxidase-like mimic combined with a peptide binder detected the SARS-CoV-2 spike protein at 8.01 pg/mL, 18.7-fold lower than the gold-nanoparticle version of the same peptide strip (DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.3c02821). Bimetallic Fe-Co particles were used for porcine rotavirus on faecal samples (100 TCID50/mL, 2-fold better than commercial gold strips; sensitivity 95.45%, specificity 100%) (DOI: 10.1016/j.ab.2026.116259), a nanozyme strip was developed for avian leukosis virus (12.5 TCID50/mL, no cross-reaction with six avian viruses) (DOI: 10.1016/j.tvjl.2025.106508), 3D magnetic multi-metallic nanozymes for influenza A reached 0.8 pg/mL, 125-fold below a commercial colloidal-gold strip, with accuracy 100% (DOI: 10.1002/exp.20240425), and a three-dimensional film-like GO-Pt-AuPt nanozyme detected two respiratory viruses at 1 pg/mL (DOI: 10.1021/acsnano.4c09513) (Table C1d). The limit addressed in 3.3.4 is the cost, stability and reactivity of the catalyst; the cost is again a substrate step.

## 3.3.5 Building the amplification into the strip

The weak point of most amplified strips is the extra step. Three designs try to make it automatic or user-friendly. A nanozyme strip for the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein with a push-button signal enhancement in a 3D-printed casing (0.1 ng/mL) worked in undiluted serum (DOI: 10.1039/d1an01350e). A strip with delayed substrate release keeps the chromogenic substrate in a glass-fibre membrane at the sample pad, so it dissolves and reaches the test line only after the nanozyme probes have been captured, which lowers the background and removes a user step (DOI: 10.1016/j.talanta.2025.127557). It was shown on a synthetic influenza A (H1N1) nucleic acid spiked into serum, with a 25-fold lower LOD than the same strip before catalysis, and has not been tested on patient samples (DOI: 10.1016/j.talanta.2025.127557). The influenza A platinum strip described in 3.3.3 uses the same idea with a timed secondary flow (DOI: 10.1021/acsinfecdis.5c00731). An aptamer-based design for the SARS-CoV-2 spike protein changes how the chemical signal is transformed to raise signal quality more than 20-fold, with a LOD of 168 pg/mL (DOI: 10.1039/d4nr04130e). These approaches aim to keep the strip one-step, which is the main reason many simple gold strips remain in use (Table C1e).

## 3.3.6 Dual-mode strips and mixed-target assays

Some nanozyme strips add a second readout, such as photothermal, fluorescent or Raman signals, or combine viral and bacterial targets. A Fe3O4@MoS2@Pt nanotag for the SARS-CoV-2 nucleoprotein and influenza A (H1N1) gave colorimetric limits of 80 and 20 ng/mL, about 100-fold better than a commercial strip, and photothermal readouts of 10 and 8 ng/mL; the photothermal LODs are not colorimetric and are kept apart in the table (DOI: 10.1021/acsami.3c15851). Others combine nanozymes with fluorescence or SERS: a SQF@ZIF-8/Pt hybrid for the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein (colorimetric 0.0302 ng/mL) (DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.5c02780), a SERS-nanozyme strip for norovirus (0.001 ng/mL visual) and adenovirus (10^2 PFU/mL visual) (DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.5c03047), and a trimodal strip for norovirus (visual 0.05 ng/mL) and E. coli O157 (DOI: 10.1021/acsami.6c07352). Three strips detect SARS-CoV-2 together with bacteria, or name only two emerging viral targets in the abstract: a magnetic nanozyme for SARS-CoV-2 and two bacteria, a Fe-DAu@Ir strip for SARS-CoV-2 and bacteria that is at least 555-fold more sensitive than a gold-nanoparticle strip, and a corona-structured magnetic nanozyme for two emerging viruses with at least 92.65-fold gain after 2 min of catalysis (DOI: 10.1002/advs.202502574; DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.5c03762; DOI: 10.1021/acs.analchem.6c03394). Only the colorimetric channel and the viral target are counted for this review (Table C1f).

## 3.3.7 What the evidence supports

For the question of this review, catalytic amplification gives the largest changes in the colour signal in the whole review. Within their own papers, the reported gains range from about 2-fold to more than 1000-fold, and the highest values come from nanozyme strips for SARS-CoV-2 (more than 1000-fold for Au@Pt, at least 555-fold for Au@Ir) and influenza A (1000-fold for the platinum strip, 125-fold for the multi-metal nanozyme), while metal deposition and natural enzymes give mostly 5- to 100-fold gains (Table C4). Nanozymes are consistently described as more stable than natural enzymes (Table C2). Three cautions run through the evidence. First, amplification is paid for with steps: substrate, hydrogen peroxide, washing or a reading device; the clearest example is the smartphone nanoenzyme sensor for the SARS-CoV-2 nucleocapsid protein, whose result depends on an optical reader. Second, the comparator differs (an unamplified strip, a commercial gold strip, the same strip before catalysis, an ELISA), so fold-improvements cannot be ranked. Third, clinical evidence is thin: where patient samples were tested, sensitivity ranged widely, from 60% for the Ebola-surrogate strip and 74% for the platinum-polymer SARS-CoV-2 strip to high values with small panels, and replicate measurements were sometimes counted as separate samples. The potato virus X and Y Fe3O4 strip shows that a nanozyme can also serve as a simple label without any amplification step, which makes the term "nanozyme strip" ambiguous in the literature.
